# Human

Several terms redirect here. For other uses, see [Human (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_\(disambiguation\)), [Mankind (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mankind_\(disambiguation\)), [Humankind (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humankind_\(disambiguation\)), [Human Race (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Race_\(disambiguation\)), [Human Being (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Being_\(disambiguation\)), and [Homo sapiens (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_sapiens_\(disambiguation\)). Not to be confused with [Person](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Person).

**Humans** (***Homo sapiens***, meaning 'thinking man' or 'wise man') are the most abundant and widespread [species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Species) of [primates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primates), characterized by [bipedalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bipedalism), minimal body hair, and large, complex brains enabling the development of advanced [technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technology), [culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture), and [language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language). Humans are highly social beings and tend to live in complex [social structures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_structure) composed of many cooperating and competing groups, from [families](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family) and [kinship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kinship) networks to political [states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_\(polity\)). [Social interactions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_interaction) between humans have established a wide variety of values, [social norms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norm_\(sociology\)), and [rituals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ritual), which bolster human [society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Society). [Curiosity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curiosity) and the human desire to understand and influence the environment have motivated humanity's development of [science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science), [philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophy), [religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion), [mythology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myth), and other fields of knowledge.

Humans have a large and highly developed [prefrontal cortex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prefrontal_cortex), the region of the brain associated with higher [cognition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognition). They are intelligent beings, capable of [episodic memory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episodic_memory#In_animals), flexible [facial expressions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facial_expression), [self-awareness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-awareness) and a [theory of mind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theory_of_mind). The human mind is capable of [introspection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Introspection) ([metacognition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metacognition)), private [thought](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thought), [imagination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imagination), [volition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volition_\(psychology\)), and forming views on [existence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Existence). Humans can also mentally travel through time ([chronesthesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chronesthesia)), which signifies [episodic foresight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foresight_\(psychology\)).[^cite_note-1] These attributes have allowed them to achieve large [technological advancements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technological_advancement) through [reason](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reason) and the transmission of [knowledge to future generations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolution_of_human_intelligence). In [anthropology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthropology), the cumulative preservation and advancement of knowledge across generations is known as the [ratchet effect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ratchet_effect).[^cite_note-\:0-2]

Humans are [omnivorous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omnivorous), capable of consuming a wide variety of plant and animal material, and have [used fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Control_of_fire_by_early_humans) to prepare and [cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cooking) food. They can survive for up to eight weeks without food, and three or four days without water. Humans are generally [diurnal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diurnality), sleeping on average seven to nine hours per day. Human [childbirth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Childbirth) is dangerous, with a high risk of complications and death. Both the mother and the father typically provide care for human offspring, who are [helpless at birth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precociality_and_altriciality).

[Genes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gene) and the [environment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environment_\(biophysical\)) influence [human biological variation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_variability) in [appearance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_physical_appearance), [physiology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physiology), [immune system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immune_system), [mental abilities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_intelligence), [body size](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_height), and [lifespan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_expectancy). Though humans vary in many traits, any two humans are on average over 99% genetically similar, with the most genetically diverse populations from [Africa](5334607.md). The greatest degree of genetic variation exists between males and females. The genetic difference between [males](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Man) and [females](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woman) is around 1% to 2%. On average, males have greater [body strength](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physical_strength) and females generally have a higher [body fat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Body_fat) percentage. Females undergo [menopause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menopause) and become [infertile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infertility) potentially decades before the end of their lives. They also have a [longer life span](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_differences_in_longevity) in almost every population around the world. The division into male and female [gender roles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_role) has varied historically, and challenges to predominant gender norms have recurred in many societies.

[Humans evolved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_evolution) from other [hominins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hominini) in Africa several million years ago. Although some scientists equate humans with all members of the genus *[Homo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo)*, in common usage it generally refers to *Homo sapiens*, the only [extant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extant_taxon) member. *Homo sapiens* [emerged](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_modern_human) around 300,000 years ago and migrated [out of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recent_African_origin_of_modern_humans), gradually replacing local populations of [archaic humans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaic_humans). Early humans were [hunter-gatherers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter-gatherer), before the [invention of agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic_Revolution) and [domestication of animals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestication_of_vertebrates) led to permanent settlement in the [Fertile Crescent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertile_Crescent) and other [cradles of civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cradle_of_civilization). As food surpluses enabled populations to become [larger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_growth) and [denser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urbanization), forms of [governance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governance) developed, the use of [writing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Writing) became necessary, and a number of [civilizations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civilization) rose and fell. Humans have continued to expand, with over 8.3 billion humans occupying almost all regions of Earth in 2026, and have even [visited the Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_landing). This expansion, combined with [industrialization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrialisation), has led to [environmental destruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_destruction) and [pollution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pollution) that significantly contributes to the ongoing [mass extinction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocene_extinction) of many other forms of life.

## Etymology and definition

Further information: [Names for the human species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_for_the_human_species) and [Human taxonomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_taxonomy)



[Carl Linnaeus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Linnaeus) coined the name *Homo sapiens*.

All modern humans are classified into the [species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Species) *Homo sapiens*, coined by [Carl Linnaeus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Linnaeus) in his 1735 work *[Systema Naturae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Systema_Naturae)*.[^cite_note-Spamer1999-3] The [generic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Name_of_a_biological_genus) name *[Homo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo)* is a learned 18th-century derivation from Latin *homō*, which refers to humans of either sex.[^cite_note-4][^cite_note-5] The word *human* can refer to all members of the *Homo* genus.[^cite_note-Barras-2016-6] The name *Homo sapiens* means 'wise man' or 'knowledgeable man'.[^cite_note-Spamer1999-3] There is [disagreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Species#The_species_problem) about whether certain extinct members of the genus, namely [Neanderthals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neanderthal), should be included as a separate species of humans or as a [subspecies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subspecies) of *H. sapiens*.[^cite_note-Barras-2016-6]

*Human* is a [loanword](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loanword) of [Middle English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_English) from [Old French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_French) *humain*, ultimately from [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin) *hūmānus*, the adjectival form of *homō* ('man' – in the sense of humanity).[^cite_note-7] The native English term *[man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Man_\(word\))* can refer to the species generally (a synonym for *humanity*) as well as to human males. It may also refer to individuals of either sex.[^cite_note-8]

Although the word *animal* is colloquially used as an antonym for *human*,[^cite_note-9] and contrary to a [common biological misconception](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_common_misconceptions_about_science,_technology,_and_mathematics#Evolution_and_paleontology), humans are [animals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal).[^cite_note-10] The word *[person](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Person)* is often used interchangeably with *human*, but philosophical debate exists as to whether [personhood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Personhood) applies to all humans or all [sentient beings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sentient_being), and further if a human can lose personhood (such as by going into a [persistent vegetative state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persistent_vegetative_state)) and what the [beginning of human personhood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beginning_of_human_personhood) is.[^cite_note-11]

## Evolution

Main article: [Human evolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_evolution)

Humans belong to the biological [family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_\(taxonomy\)) of great apes (family [Hominidae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hominidae), [superfamily Hominoidea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hominoidea)).[^cite_note-12] The [lineage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lineage_\(evolution\)) of apes that eventually gave rise to humans first split from [gibbons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gibbon) (family Hylobatidae), next [orangutans](22433.md) (genus *Pongo*), then [gorillas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gorilla) (genus *Gorilla*), and finally, [chimpanzees](7844.md) and [bonobos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonobo) (genus *[Pan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan_\(genus\))*). The last split, between the human and chimpanzee–bonobo lineages, took place around 4-8 million years ago, in the late [Miocene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miocene) epoch.[^cite_note-13][^cite_note-14] During this split, [chromosome 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chromosome_2) was formed in humans from the joining of two other chromosomes, leaving humans with only 23 pairs of chromosomes, compared to 24 for the other apes.[^cite_note-15] Following their split with chimpanzees and bonobos, the [hominins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hominini) diversified into many species and at least two distinct genera. All but one of these lineages – representing the genus *[Homo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo)* and its sole extant species *Homo sapiens* – are now extinct.[^cite_note-16]



Reconstruction of [Lucy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucy_\(hominid\))*,* the first *[Australopithecus afarensis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australopithecus_afarensis)* skeleton found

The genus *Homo* evolved from *[Australopithecus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australopithecus)*.[^cite_note-17][^cite_note-18] Though [fossils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_fossils) from the transition are scarce, the earliest members of *Homo* share several key traits with *Australopithecus*.[^cite_note-19][^cite_note-Villmoare2015-20] Due to the scant available evidence, the time of the divergence to the genus *Homo* does not have a consensus.[^cite_note-Wood-2011-21] Some studies using [molecular clock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molecular_clock) techniques estimate the *Homo* genus appeared 4.30–2.56 million years ago,[^cite_note-22] while others contest that some early *Homo* species are incorrectly included in the genus and therefore put this estimate at about 1.87 million years ago.[^cite_note-Wood-2011-21]

The earliest record of *Homo* is the 2.8 million-year-old specimen [LD 350-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LD_350-1) from [Ethiopia](187749.md), and the earliest named species are *[Homo habilis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_habilis)* and *[Homo rudolfensis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_rudolfensis),* which evolved by 2.3 million years ago.[^cite_note-Villmoare2015-20] *[H. erectus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_erectus)* (the African variant is sometimes called *[H. ergaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_ergaster)*) evolved 2 million years ago and was the first [archaic human](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaic_human) species to leave Africa and disperse across Eurasia.[^cite_note-23] *H. erectus* also was the first to evolve a characteristically human [body plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Body_plan). *Homo sapiens* emerged in Africa at least 300,000 years ago from a species commonly designated as either *[H. heidelbergensis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_heidelbergensis)* or *[H. rhodesiensis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_rhodesiensis)*, the descendants of *H. erectus* that remained in Africa.[^cite_note-24] *H. sapiens* migrated out of the continent, gradually replacing or interbreeding with local populations of archaic humans.[^cite_note-25][^cite_note-26][^cite_note-27] Humans began exhibiting [behavioral modernity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Behavioral_modernity) about 160,000–70,000 years ago,[^cite_note-28] and possibly earlier.[^cite_note-29] This development was likely selected amidst [natural climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_variability_and_change) in [Middle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Pleistocene) to [Late Pleistocene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Pleistocene) Africa.[^cite_note-30]

The ["out of Africa" migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recent_African_origin_of_modern_humans) took place in at least two waves, the first around 130,000 to 100,000 years ago, the second ([Southern Dispersal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Dispersal)) around 70,000 to 50,000 years ago.[^cite_note-31][^cite_note-32] *H. sapiens* proceeded to colonize all the continents and larger islands [except Antarctica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonization_of_Antarctica), arriving in [Eurasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasia) 125,000 years ago,[^cite_note-33][^cite_note-34] Australia around 65,000 years ago,[^cite_note-35] the Americas around 15,000 years ago, and remote islands such as [Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_Islands), [Easter Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Easter_Island), [Madagascar](18964.md), and [New Zealand](4913064.md) in the years 300 to 1280 CE.[^cite_note-36][^cite_note-37]

Human evolution was not a simple linear or branched progression but involved [interbreeding between related species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interbreeding_between_archaic_and_modern_humans).[^cite_note-pmid21179161-38][^cite_note-39][^cite_note-40] Genomic research has shown that hybridization between substantially diverged lineages was common in human evolution.[^cite_note-41] [DNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DNA) evidence suggests that several genes of Neanderthal origin are present among all non-sub-Saharan African populations, and Neanderthals and other hominins, such as [Denisovans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denisovan), may have contributed up to 6% of their [genome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genome) to present-day non-sub-Saharan African humans.[^cite_note-pmid21179161-38][^cite_note-42][^cite_note-43]

Human evolution is characterized by a number of [morphological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morphology_\(biology\)), [developmental](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_development_\(biology\)), [physiological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_physiology), and [behavioral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_behavior) changes that have taken place since the split between the [last common ancestor of humans and chimpanzees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chimpanzee%E2%80%93human_last_common_ancestor). The most significant of these adaptations are [hairlessness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistory_of_nakedness_and_clothing#Evolution_of_hairlessness),[^cite_note-Sandel2013-44] obligate bipedalism, increased brain size, and decreased [sexual dimorphism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_dimorphism) ([neoteny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoteny)). The relationship between all these changes is the subject of ongoing debate.[^cite_note-45]

| [Hominoidea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hominoidea) (hominoids, [apes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ape)) | Hylobatidae ([gibbons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gibbon))    Hominidae (hominids, [great apes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_ape))     [Ponginae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ponginae)     Pongo ([orangutans](22433.md))       *[Pongo abelii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pongo_abelii)*           *[Pongo tapanuliensis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pongo_tapanuliensis)*      *[Pongo pygmaeus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pongo_pygmaeus)*             [Homininae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homininae) (hominines)     [Gorillini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gorillini)     Gorilla ([gorillas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gorilla))       *[Gorilla gorilla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gorilla_gorilla)*      *[Gorilla beringei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gorilla_beringei)*          [Hominini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hominini) (hominins)     [Panina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panina)     Pan ([chimpanzees](7844.md))       *[Pan troglodytes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan_troglodytes)*      *[Pan paniscus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan_paniscus)*               [Hominina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hominina) (homininans)  ***Homo sapiens*** (humans) |

## History

Main article: [Human history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_history)

### Prehistory

Main article: [Prehistory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistory)



Overview of the peopling of the world by [early human migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_human_migrations) during the [Upper Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Paleolithic), following the [Southern Dispersal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Dispersal) paradigm

Until about 12,000 years ago, all humans lived as [hunter-gatherers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter-gatherer).[^cite_note-46][^cite_note-47] The [Neolithic Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic_Revolution) (the invention of [agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture)) first [took place](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Origins_of_agriculture_in_West_Asia) in [Southwest Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwest_Asia) and spread through large parts of the [Old World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_World) over the following millennia.[^cite_note-48] It also occurred independently in [Mesoamerica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesoamerica) (about 6,000 years ago),[^cite_note-49] China,[^cite_note-50][^cite_note-51] [Papua New Guinea](22943.md),[^cite_note-52] and the [Sahel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahel) and [West Savanna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudanian_savanna) regions of Africa.[^cite_note-53][^cite_note-54][^cite_note-55]

The formation of permanent [human settlements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_settlement), the [domestication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestication) of animals, and the [use of metal tools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chalcolithic) were followed by a permanent food surplus, for the first time in history. Agriculture and a sedentary lifestyle led to the emergence of early civilizations.[^cite_note-56][^cite_note-57][^cite_note-58]

### Ancient

Main article: [Ancient history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_history)



[Great Pyramids of Giza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giza_pyramid_complex), Egypt

An [urban revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_revolution) took place in the 4th millennium BCE with the development of [city-states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City-state), particularly [Sumerian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumer) cities located in [Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamia).[^cite_note-59] It was in these cities that the earliest known form of writing, [cuneiform script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuneiform_script), appeared around 3000 BCE.[^cite_note-60] Other major civilizations to develop around this time were [Ancient Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egypt) and the [Indus Valley Civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_Valley_Civilization).[^cite_note-61] They eventually traded with each other and invented technology such as wheels, plows and sails.[^cite_note-62][^cite_note-Bodnár-2018-63][^cite_note-64][^cite_note-65] Emerging by 3000 BCE, the [Caral–Supe civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caral%E2%80%93Supe_civilization) is the oldest complex civilization in the Americas.[^cite_note-66] Astronomy and mathematics were also developed, and the [Great Pyramid of Giza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Pyramid_of_Giza) was built.[^cite_note-67][^cite_note-68][^cite_note-69] There is evidence of a [severe drought](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/4.2-kiloyear_event) lasting about a hundred years that may have caused the decline of these civilizations,[^cite_note-70] with new ones appearing in the aftermath. [Babylonians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylonia) came to dominate Mesopotamia while others[^cite_note-71]—such as the [Poverty Point culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_Point_culture), [Minoans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minoan_civilization), and the [Shang dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shang_dynasty)—rose to prominence in new areas.[^cite_note-72][^cite_note-73][^cite_note-74] The [Late Bronze Age collapse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Bronze_Age_collapse) around 1200 BCE resulted in the disappearance of a number of civilizations and the beginning of the [Greek Dark Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Dark_Ages).[^cite_note-75][^cite_note-76] During this period iron started replacing bronze, leading to the [Iron Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Age).[^cite_note-77]

In the 5th century BCE, history started being [recorded as a discipline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historiography), which provided a much clearer picture of life at the time.[^cite_note-78] Between the 8th and 6th centuries BCE, Europe entered the [classical antiquity](252905.md) age, a period when [ancient Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece) and [ancient Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Rome) flourished.[^cite_note-79][^cite_note-80] Around this time, other civilizations also came to prominence. The [Maya civilization](18449273.md) started to build cities and create [complex calendars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maya_calendar).[^cite_note-81][^cite_note-82] In Africa, the [Kingdom of Aksum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Aksum) overtook the declining [Kingdom of Kush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kush) and facilitated trade between India and the Mediterranean.[^cite_note-83] In West Asia, the [Achaemenid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire)'s system of centralized governance became the precursor to many later empires,[^cite_note-84] while the [Gupta Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gupta_Empire) in India and the [Han dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Han_dynasty) in China have been described as [golden ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_ages) in their respective regions.[^cite_note-85][^cite_note-86]

### Post-classical

Main article: [Post-classical history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-classical_history)



Medieval French [manuscript illustration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illuminated_manuscript) of the three [classes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_class) of medieval society from the 13th-century *Li Livres dou Santé*

Following the [fall of the Western Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_the_Western_Roman_Empire) in 476, Europe entered the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages).[^cite_note-87] During this period, [Christianity](5211.md) and the [Church](606848.md) would act as a source of authority and education.[^cite_note-88] In the Middle East, [Islam](6037917.md) became the prominent religion and expanded into North Africa. It led to an [Islamic Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Golden_Age), inspiring achievements in [architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture), the revival of old advances in science and technology, and the formation of a distinct way of life.[^cite_note-Renima-2016-89][^cite_note-90] The [Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christendom) and [Islamic worlds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_world) would eventually clash, with the [Kingdom of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_England), the [Kingdom of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_France), and the [Holy Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Roman_Empire) declaring a series of [holy wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crusades) to regain control of the [Holy Land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Land) from [Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim).[^cite_note-91]

In the Americas, between 200 and 900 CE, [Mesoamerica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesoamerica) was in its [Classic Period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesoamerican_Classic_period),[^cite_note-92] while further north, complex [Mississippian societies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippian_culture) would arise starting around 800 CE.[^cite_note-93] The [Mongol Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_Empire) would conquer much of [Eurasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasia) in the 13th and 14th centuries.[^cite_note-94] Over this same time period, the [Mali Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mali_Empire) in Africa grew to be the largest empire on the continent, stretching from [Senegambia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senegambia) to [Ivory Coast](57744.md).[^cite_note-95] Oceania would see the rise of the [Tuʻi Tonga Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tu%CA%BBi_Tonga_Empire), which expanded across many islands in the South Pacific.[^cite_note-96] By the late 15th century, the [Aztecs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aztecs) and [Inca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inca) had become the dominant power in Mesoamerica and the [Andes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andean_civilizations), respectively.[^cite_note-97]

### Modern

Main article: [Modern era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_era)

The [early modern period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_modern_period) in Europe and the Near East (c. 1450–1800) began with the [final defeat of the Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Constantinople) and the [rise of the Ottoman Empire](22278.md).[^cite_note-98] Meanwhile, Japan entered the [Edo period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edo_period),[^cite_note-99] the [Qing dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qing_dynasty) rose in China,[^cite_note-100] and the [Mughal Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_Empire) ruled much of India.[^cite_note-101] Europe underwent the [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance), starting in the 15th century,[^cite_note-102] and the [Age of Discovery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Discovery) began with the exploration and [colonization](7299.md) of new regions.[^cite_note-103] This included the [colonization of the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_colonization_of_the_Americas)[^cite_note-104] and the [Columbian Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbian_Exchange).[^cite_note-105] This expansion led to the [Atlantic slave trade](49005.md)[^cite_note-106] and the [genocide of the Americas' indigenous peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genocide_of_indigenous_people_in_the_Americas).[^cite_note-107] This period also marked the [Scientific Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_Revolution), with great advances in [mathematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathematics), [mechanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mechanics), [astronomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astronomy), and [physiology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physiology).[^cite_note-108]



[Buzz Aldrin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buzz_Aldrin) on the Moon during the 1969 [Apollo 11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_11) mission, as he and [Neil Armstrong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neil_Armstrong) became the first humans to set foot on another [celestial body](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celestial_body)

The [late modern period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_modern_period) (1800–present) saw the [Industrial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution) and [Technological Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technological_Revolution) bring such discoveries as [imaging technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imaging_technology), major innovations in transport, and [energy development](533487.md).[^cite_note-109] Influenced by [Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment) ideals, the Americas and Europe experienced a period of political revolutions known as the [Age of Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Revolution).[^cite_note-110] The [Napoleonic Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars) raged through Europe in the early 1800s,[^cite_note-111] Spain lost most of its colonies in the [New World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_World),[^cite_note-112] while Europeans continued [expansion into Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scramble_for_Africa) – where European control went from 10% to almost 90% in less than 50 years[^cite_note-113] – and Oceania.[^cite_note-114] In the 19th century, the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) expanded to become the [world's largest empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_empires).[^cite_note-115]

A tenuous [balance of power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balance_of_power_\(international_relations\)) among European nations collapsed in 1914 with the outbreak of the [First World War](4764461.md), one of the deadliest conflicts in history.[^cite_note-116] In the 1930s, [a worldwide economic crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression) led to the rise of [authoritarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarian) regimes and a [Second World War](32927.md), involving [almost all of the world's countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II_by_country).[^cite_note-117] The war's destruction led to the collapse of most global empires, leading to widespread decolonization.

Following the conclusion of the Second World War in 1945, the [United States](3434750.md)[^cite_note-118] and the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) emerged as the remaining [global superpowers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_superpower). This led to a [Cold War](325329.md) that saw a struggle for global influence, including a [nuclear arms race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_arms_race) and a [space race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_Race), ending in the collapse of the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-119][^cite_note-120] The current [Information Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_Age), spurred by the development of the [Internet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet) and [artificial intelligence](1164.md) systems, sees the world becoming increasingly [globalized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalization) and interconnected.[^cite_note-121]

## Habitat and population

Further information: [Human geography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_geography) and [Demography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demography)

| [Choropleth map](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Choropleth_map) showing [Population density](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_density) (people per square kilometer) estimates by 30 arc-second grid in 2020 |  |
| --- | --- |
| [World population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_population) | ≈ 8.3 billion |
| [Population density](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_density#Human_population_density) | 16/km<sup>2</sup> (42/sq mi) by total area 55/km<sup>2</sup> (143/sq mi) by land area |
| [Largest cities](14649921.md)[^cite_note-126] | [Tokyo](30057.md), [Delhi](37756.md), [Shanghai](27643.md), [São Paulo](390875.md), [Mexico City](18987.md), [Cairo](6293.md), [Mumbai](19189.md), [Beijing](18603746.md), [Dhaka](56656.md), [Osaka](52230.md) |

Early human settlements were dependent on proximity to [water](18581463.md) and – depending on the lifestyle – other [natural resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_resource) used for [subsistence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subsistence), such as populations of animal prey for [hunting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunting) and [arable land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arable_land) for growing crops and grazing livestock.[^cite_note-127] Modern humans, however, have a great capacity for altering their [habitats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habitat_\(ecology\)) by means of technology, [irrigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irrigation), [urban planning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_planning), construction, [deforestation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deforestation) and [desertification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desertification).[^cite_note-128] [Human settlements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_settlements) continue to be [vulnerable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vulnerability) to [natural disasters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_disasters), especially those placed in hazardous locations and with low quality of construction.[^cite_note-129] Grouping and deliberate habitat alteration are often done with the goals of providing protection, accumulating comforts or material wealth, expanding the available food, improving [aesthetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aesthetics), increasing knowledge, or enhancing the exchange of resources.[^cite_note-130]

Humans are one of the most [adaptable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adaptation) species, despite having a low or narrow tolerance for many of the Earth's extreme environments.[^cite_note-Piantadosi-2003-131] Currently the species is present in all eight [biogeographical realms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biogeographical_realms), although their presence in the [Antarctic realm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antarctic_realm) is limited to [research stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_stations_in_Antarctica), and annually there is a population decline in the winter months of this realm.[^cite_note-132] Humans have had a dramatic [effect on the environment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_impact_on_the_environment). Human population growth, [industrialization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrialization), [land development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_development), [overconsumption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overconsumption), and combustion of [fossil fuels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil_fuels) have led to [environmental destruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_destruction) and [pollution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pollution) that significantly contributes to the ongoing [mass extinction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocene_extinction) of other forms of life.[^cite_note-133][^cite_note-134]

Within the last century, humans have also explored the [deep sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deep_sea) and [outer space](177602.md). Human habitation within these hostile environments is restrictive and expensive, typically limited in duration, and restricted to [scientific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific), [military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military), or [industrial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industry_\(economics\)) expeditions.[^cite_note-135] Humans [have visited](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exploration_of_the_Moon) the [Moon](19331.md) and made their presence known on other [celestial bodies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celestial_bodies) through human-made [robotic spacecraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robotic_spacecraft).[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137][^cite_note-138] Since 2000, there has been continuous human presence [in space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_presence_in_space) through habitation on the [International Space Station](15043.md).[^cite_note-139]

By using advanced tools and [clothing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clothing), humans have been able to extend their tolerance to a wide variety of temperatures, [humidities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humidity), and altitudes.[^cite_note-Piantadosi-2003-131][^cite_note-140] As a result, humans are a [cosmopolitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmopolitan_distribution) species found in almost all regions of the world, including [tropical rainforests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_rainforest), [arid deserts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desert), extremely cold [Arctic regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arctic_region), and heavily polluted cities; in comparison, most other species are confined to a few geographical areas by their limited adaptability.[^cite_note-141] The [human population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_population) is not, however, uniformly distributed on the [Earth](9228.md)'s surface, because the population density varies from one region to another, and large stretches of surface are almost completely uninhabited, like [Antarctica](18959138.md) and vast swathes of the ocean.[^cite_note-Piantadosi-2003-131][^cite_note-142] Most humans (61%) live in Asia; the remainder live in the Americas (14%), Africa (14%), Europe (11%), and Oceania (0.5%).[^cite_note-143]



&#x20;Humans and their domesticated animals represent 96% of all mammalian biomass on Earth, whereas all wild mammals represent only 4%.

[^cite_note-Bar-On-144]

Estimates of the population at the time agriculture emerged in around 10,000 BC have ranged between 1 million and 15 million.[^cite_note-145][^cite_note-146] Around 50–60 million people lived in the combined eastern and western [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) in the 4th century AD.[^cite_note-147] [Bubonic plagues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bubonic_plague), first recorded in the 6th century AD, reduced the population by 50%, with the [Black Death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Death) killing 75–200 million people in [Eurasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasia) and [North Africa](21714.md) alone.[^cite_note-148] The human population is believed to have reached one billion in 1800. It has since then increased exponentially, reaching two billion in 1930 and three billion in 1960, four in 1975, five in 1987, and six billion in 1999.[^cite_note-149] It passed seven billion in 2011[^cite_note-150] and passed eight billion in 2022.[^cite_note-151] It took over two million years of [human prehistory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistory) and [history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_history) for the human population to reach one [billion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billion), and only 207 years more to grow to 7 billion.[^cite_note-152] The combined [biomass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biomass_\(ecology\)) of the carbon of all the humans on Earth in 2018 was estimated at 60 million tons, about 10 times larger than that of all non-domesticated mammals.[^cite_note-Bar-On-144]

In 2018, 4.2 billion humans (55%) lived in urban areas, up from 751 million in 1950.[^cite_note-United_Nations_Department_of_Economic_and_Social_Affairs_(DESA)-2018-153] The most urbanized regions are Northern America (82%), Latin America (81%), Europe (74%), and Oceania (68%), with Africa and Asia having nearly 90% of the world's 3.4 billion rural population.[^cite_note-United_Nations_Department_of_Economic_and_Social_Affairs_(DESA)-2018-153] Problems for humans living in cities include various forms of pollution and [crime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime),[^cite_note-154] especially in inner-city and suburban [slums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slum).

## Biology

### Anatomy and physiology



Basic anatomical features of female and male humans. These models have had [body hair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Body_hair) and male [facial hair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facial_hair) removed and head hair trimmed.

Main article: [Human body](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_body)

Most aspects of human physiology are closely [homologous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homology_\(biology\)) to corresponding aspects of animal physiology. The [dental formula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dental_formula) of humans is: 2.1.2.32.1.2.3, like other [catarrhines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catarrhini). Humans have proportionately shorter [palates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palate) and much smaller [teeth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_tooth) than other primates. They are the only primates to have short, relatively flush [canine teeth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canine_teeth). Humans have characteristically crowded teeth, with gaps from lost teeth usually closing up quickly in young individuals. Humans are gradually losing their [third molars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wisdom_teeth), with some individuals having them congenitally absent.[^cite_note-Revolution-155]

Humans share with chimpanzees a [vestigial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vestigiality) tail,[^cite_note-156] [appendix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appendix_\(anatomy\)), flexible shoulder joints, grasping fingers and [opposable thumbs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opposable_thumb).[^cite_note-157] Humans also have a more barrel-shaped chest in contrast to the funnel shape of other apes, an adaptation for bipedal respiration.[^cite_note-158] Apart from bipedalism and brain size, humans differ from chimpanzees mostly in [smelling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smelling), [hearing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hearing), and [digesting proteins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digestion#Protein_digestion).[^cite_note-O'Neil-159] While humans have a density of [hair follicles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hair_follicle) comparable to other apes, it is predominantly [vellus hair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vellus_hair), most of which is so short and wispy as to be practically invisible.[^cite_note-160][^cite_note-Sandel2013-44] Humans have about 2 million [sweat glands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sweat_gland) spread over their entire bodies, many more than chimpanzees, whose sweat glands are scarce and are mainly located on the palm of the hand and on the soles of the feet.[^cite_note-161]

It is estimated that the worldwide average [height for an adult human](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_height) male is about 171 cm (5 ft 7 in), while the worldwide average height for adult human females is about 159 cm (5 ft 3 in).[^cite_note-162] Shrinkage of stature may begin in middle age in some individuals but tends to be typical in the extremely [aged](229060.md).[^cite_note-163] Throughout history, human populations have universally become taller, probably as a consequence of better nutrition, healthcare, and living conditions.[^cite_note-164] The average [mass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Body_weight) of an adult human is 59 kg (130 lb) for females and 77 kg (170 lb) for males.[^cite_note-165] Like many other conditions, body weight and body type are influenced by both [genetic susceptibility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_predisposition) and environment and vary greatly among individuals.[^cite_note-166][^cite_note-167]

Humans have a far faster and more accurate [throw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Throw) than other animals.[^cite_note-168] Humans are also among the best long-distance runners in the animal kingdom, but slower over short distances.[^cite_note-169][^cite_note-O'Neil-159] Humans' thinner body hair and more productive sweat glands help avoid [heat exhaustion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heat_exhaustion) while running for long distances.[^cite_note-170] Compared to other apes, the human [heart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heart) produces greater [stroke volume](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stroke_volume) and [cardiac output](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardiac_output), and the [aorta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aorta) is proportionately larger.[^cite_note-171][^cite_note-172]

### Genetics

Main article: [Human genetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_genetics)



A graphical representation of the standard human [karyotype](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karyotype), including both the female (XX) and male (XY) sex chromosomes

Humans are, like all animals, [plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plant), and [fungi](19178965.md), a [eukaryotic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eukaryote) species, and like most animals, a [diploid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ploidy) species. Each [somatic cell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Somatic_cell) has two sets of 23 [chromosomes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chromosome), each set received from one parent; [gametes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamete) have only one set of chromosomes, which is a mixture of the two parental sets. Among the 23 pairs of chromosomes, there are 22 pairs of [autosomes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autosome) and one pair of [sex chromosomes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex-determination_system). In humans, sex determination is primarily mediated by the [SRY gene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex-determining_region_Y_protein) located on the Y chromosome. While the typical configuration follows an [XY sex-determination system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/XY_sex-determination_system) (XX for females, XY for males), the genetic expression of the Sex-determining Region Y protein is the critical factor in initiating male gonadal differentiation.[^cite_note-173] Consequently, individuals may be born with a chromosomal [genotype](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genotype) that does not align with their [phenotypic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phenotype) sex, a condition often resulting in infertility due to the absence of specific genes required for [gametogenesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gametogenesis).[^cite_note-174] [Genes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gene) and [environment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environment_\(biophysical\)) influence human biological variation in visible characteristics, physiology, disease susceptibility and mental abilities. The exact influence of genes and environment on certain traits is not well understood.[^cite_note-175][^cite_note-176]

While no humans – not even [monozygotic twins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monozygotic_twins) – are genetically identical,[^cite_note-177] two humans on average will have a genetic similarity of 99.5%-99.9%.[^cite_note-genetic_variation1-178][^cite_note-179] This makes them more [homogeneous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_genetic_variation) than other great apes, including chimpanzees.[^cite_note-REGWG2005-180][^cite_note-181] This small variation in human DNA compared to many other species suggests a [population bottleneck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_bottleneck) during the [Late Pleistocene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Pleistocene) (around 100,000 years ago), in which the human population was reduced to a small number of breeding pairs.[^cite_note-182][^cite_note-183] The forces of [natural selection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_selection) have continued to operate on human populations, with evidence that certain regions of the [genome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genome) display [directional selection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Directional_selection) in the past 15,000 years.[^cite_note-184]

In 1984, the US government began to plan the [Human Genome Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Genome_Project) (HGP), which officially started in 1990.[^cite_note-185] Utilizing the data from the HGP, 92% of the [human genome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_genome) was first sequenced in 2001.[^cite_note-186] After the HGP was completed in April 2003, the [All of Us Research Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_of_Us_\(initiative\)) in 2022 released its first major dataset, which included nearly 100,000 whole genome sequences. By April 2023, approximately 245,000 genomes had been sequenced.[^cite_note-187] In 2012, the [International HapMap Project](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_HapMap_Project) had compared the genomes of 1,184 individuals from 11 populations and identified 1.6 million [single nucleotide polymorphisms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single_nucleotide_polymorphisms).[^cite_note-188] African populations harbor the highest number of private genetic variants. While many of the common variants found in populations outside of Africa are also found on the African continent, there are still large numbers that are private to these regions, especially [Oceania](22621.md) and [the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Americas).[^cite_note-Bergstrom2020-189] By 2010 estimates, humans have approximately 22,000 genes.[^cite_note-190] By comparing [mitochondrial DNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MtDNA), which is inherited only from the mother, geneticists have concluded that the last female common ancestor whose [genetic marker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_marker) is found in all modern humans, the so-called [mitochondrial Eve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitochondrial_Eve), must have lived around 90,000 to 200,000 years ago.[^cite_note-191][^cite_note-192][^cite_note-193][^cite_note-194]

### Life cycle

See also: [Childbirth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Childbirth) and [Life expectancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_expectancy)



A 10 mm [human embryo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_embryo) at 5 weeks

Most [human reproduction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_reproduction) takes place by [internal fertilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_fertilization) via [sexual intercourse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_sexual_intercourse), but can also occur through [assisted reproductive technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assisted_reproductive_technology) procedures.[^cite_note-195] The average [gestation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gestation) period is 38 weeks, but a normal pregnancy can vary by up to 37 days.[^cite_note-196] Embryonic development in humans covers the first eight weeks of development; at the beginning of the ninth week the embryo is termed a [fetus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fetus).[^cite_note-197] Humans are able to [induce early labor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labor_induction) or perform a [caesarean section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caesarean_section) if the child needs to be born earlier for medical reasons.[^cite_note-198] In developed countries, [infants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infant) are typically 3–4 kg (7–9 lb) in weight and 47–53 cm (19–21 in) in height at birth.[^cite_note-199][^cite_note-200] However, [low birth weight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low_birth_weight) is common in developing countries, and it contributes to the high levels of [infant mortality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infant_mortality) in these regions.[^cite_note-201]

Compared with other species, human childbirth is dangerous, with a much higher risk of complications and death.[^cite_note-202] The size of the fetus's head is more closely matched to the [pelvis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pelvis) than in other primates.[^cite_note-Pavlicev-203] The reason for this is not completely understood,[^cite_note-205] but it contributes to a painful labor that can last 24 hours or more.[^cite_note-206] The chances of a successful labor increased significantly during the 20th century in wealthier countries with the advent of new medical technologies. In contrast, pregnancy and [natural childbirth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_childbirth) remain hazardous ordeals in developing regions of the world, with [maternal death rates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maternal_death_rates) approximately 100 times greater than in developed countries.[^cite_note-207]

Both the mother and the father provide care for human offspring, in contrast to other primates, where parental care is mostly done by the mother.[^cite_note-208] [Helpless at birth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altricial), humans continue to grow for some years, typically reaching [sexual maturity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_maturity) at 15 to 17 years of age.[^cite_note-209][^cite_note-210][^cite_note-211] The human life span has been split into various stages ranging from three to twelve. Common stages include [infancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infant), [childhood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Childhood), [adolescence](83859.md), [adulthood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adult) and [old age](229060.md).[^cite_note-212] The lengths of these stages have varied across cultures and time periods but are typified by an unusually rapid growth spurt during adolescence.[^cite_note-213] Human females undergo [menopause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menopause) and become [infertile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infertility) at around the age of 50.[^cite_note-214] It has been proposed that menopause increases a woman's overall reproductive success by allowing her to invest more time and resources in her existing offspring, and in turn their children (the [grandmother hypothesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grandmother_hypothesis)), rather than by continuing to bear children into old age.[^cite_note-215][^cite_note-216]

The life span of an individual depends on two major factors, genetics and lifestyle choices.[^cite_note-217] For various reasons, including biological/genetic causes, women live on average about four years longer than men.[^cite_note-218] As of 2018, the global average [life expectancy at birth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_expectancy_at_birth) of a girl is estimated to be 74.9 years compared to 70.4 for a boy.[^cite_note-219][^cite_note-220] There are significant geographical variations in human life expectancy, mostly correlated with economic development – for example, life expectancy at birth in [Hong Kong](13404.md) is 87.6 years for girls and 81.8 for boys, while in the [Central African Republic](5478.md), it is 55.0 years for girls and 50.6 for boys.[^cite_note-221][^cite_note-222] The developed world is generally aging, with the median age around 40 years. In the [developing world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_world), the median age is between 15 and 20 years. While one in five Europeans is 60 years of age or older, only one in twenty Africans is 60 years of age or older.[^cite_note-223] In 2012, the United Nations estimated that there were 316,600 living [centenarians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centenarians) (humans of age 100 or older) worldwide.[^cite_note-224]

|  |  |  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  |  |  |  |  |
| [Infant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infant) boy and girl | Boy and girl before [puberty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puberty) ([children](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child)) | [Adolescent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adolescent) male and female | [Adult](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adult) man and woman | [Elderly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elderly) man and woman |

### Diet

Main article: [Human nutrition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_nutrition)



Humans preparing a meal in [Bali](4147.md), [Indonesia](14579.md)

Humans are [omnivorous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omnivorous),[^cite_note-225] opportunistic feeders capable of consuming a wide variety of plant and animal material.[^cite_note-226][^cite_note-227] Human groups have adopted a range of diets from purely [vegan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vegan) to primarily [carnivorous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnivorous). In some cases, dietary restrictions in humans can lead to [deficiency diseases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deficiency_diseases); however, stable human groups have adapted to many dietary patterns through both genetic specialization and cultural conventions to use nutritionally balanced food sources.[^cite_note-228] The human diet is prominently reflected in human culture and has led to the development of [food science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_science).[^cite_note-Crittenden-2017-229]

Until the development of agriculture, *Homo sapiens* employed a hunter-gatherer method as their sole means of food collection.[^cite_note-Crittenden-2017-229] This involved combining stationary food sources (such as fruits, grains, tubers, and mushrooms, insect larvae, and aquatic mollusks) with [wild game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Game_\(food\)), which must be hunted and captured in order to be consumed.[^cite_note-230] It has been proposed that humans have used fire to prepare and [cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cooking) food since the time of *[Homo erectus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_erectus)*.[^cite_note-231] Human [domestication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestication) of wild plants began about 11,700 years ago, leading to the [development of agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_agriculture),[^cite_note-232] a gradual process called the [Neolithic Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic_Revolution).[^cite_note-233] These dietary changes may also have altered human biology; the spread of [dairy farming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dairy_farming) provided a new and rich source of food, leading to the evolution of the ability to digest [lactose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lactose) in some adults.[^cite_note-234][^cite_note-235] The types of food consumed, and how they are prepared, have varied widely by time, location, and culture.[^cite_note-236][^cite_note-237]

In general, humans can survive for up to eight weeks without food, depending on stored body fat.[^cite_note-238] Survival without water is usually limited to three or four days, with a maximum of one week.[^cite_note-239] In 2020, it was estimated that 9 million humans die every year from causes directly or indirectly related to [starvation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starvation).[^cite_note-240][^cite_note-241] Childhood malnutrition is also common and contributes to the [global burden of disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disease_burden).[^cite_note-242] However, global food distribution is not even, and [obesity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obesity) among some human populations has increased rapidly, leading to health complications and increased mortality in some [developed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_country) and a few [developing countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developing_countries). Worldwide, over one billion people are obese,[^cite_note-Haslam-243] while in the United States, 35% of people are obese, leading to this being described as an "[obesity epidemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epidemiology_of_obesity)".[^cite_note-244] Obesity is caused by consuming more [calories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calorie) than are expended, so excessive weight gain is usually caused by an energy-dense diet.[^cite_note-Haslam-243]

Food consumption is the first step of the [digestive process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_digestive_system), in which humans ultimately expel [feces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_feces) ranging in frequency from multiple times per day to multiple times per week.[^cite_note-245]

### Biological variation

Main article: [Human genetic variation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_genetic_variation)



A [Libyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berbers), a [Nubian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nubians), a [Syrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_people), and an [Egyptian](31912046.md), drawing by an unknown artist after a mural of the tomb of [Seti I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seti_I), copied by Heinrich von Minutoli (1820). Note that the exaggerated skin shades are due to the 19th century illustrator, not the [Ancient Egyptian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egypt) original.

There is biological variation in the human species – with traits such as [blood type](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_type), [genetic diseases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_diseases), [cranial features](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_skull), [facial features](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_face), [organ systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organ_systems), [eye color](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eye_color), [hair color](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hair_color) and [texture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hair_texture), [height](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_height) and [build](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Body_shape), and [skin color](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_skin_color) varying across the globe. The typical height of an adult human is between 1.4 and 1.9 m (4 ft 7 in and 6 ft 3 in), although this varies significantly depending on sex, [ethnic origin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_origin), and family bloodlines.[^cite_note-246][^cite_note-247] Body size is partly determined by genes and is also significantly influenced by environmental factors such as [diet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diet_\(nutrition\)), exercise, and [sleep patterns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sleep_pattern).[^cite_note-248]



A variety of human hair colors; from top left, clockwise: [black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_hair), [brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_hair), [blonde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blond), [white](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_hair), [red](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_hair).

There is evidence that populations have adapted genetically to various external factors. The genes that allow adult humans to [digest lactose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lactose_tolerance) are present in high frequencies in populations that have long histories of cattle domestication and are more dependent on [cow milk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cow_milk).[^cite_note-249] [Sickle cell anemia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sickle_cell_anemia), which may provide increased resistance to [malaria](20423.md), is frequent in populations where malaria is endemic.[^cite_note-250][^cite_note-251] Populations that have for a very long time inhabited specific climates tend to have developed specific [phenotypes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phenotype) that are beneficial for those environments – [short stature and stocky build in cold regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allen's_rule), tall and lanky in hot regions, and with high lung capacities or other [adaptations at high altitudes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-altitude_adaptation_in_humans).[^cite_note-252] Some populations have evolved highly unique adaptations to very specific environmental conditions, such as those advantageous to ocean-dwelling lifestyles and [freediving](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freediving) in the [Bajau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bajau).[^cite_note-253]

Human hair ranges in color from [red](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_hair) to [blond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blond) to [brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_hair) to [black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_hair), which is the most frequent.[^cite_note-254] Hair color depends on the amount of [melanin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melanin), with concentrations fading with increased age, leading to [grey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grey_hair) or even white hair. Skin color can range from [darkest brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dark_skin) to [lightest peach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_skin), or even nearly white or colorless in cases of [albinism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albinism).[^cite_note-roberts1-255] It tends to vary [clinally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinal_variation) and generally correlates with the level of [ultraviolet radiation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultraviolet) in a particular geographic area, with darker skin mostly around the equator.[^cite_note-Jablonski2004-256] Skin darkening may have evolved as protection against ultraviolet solar radiation.[^cite_note-257] Light skin pigmentation protects against depletion of [vitamin D](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vitamin_D), which requires [sunlight](27979.md) to make.[^cite_note-258] Human skin also has a capacity to darken (tan) in response to exposure to ultraviolet radiation.[^cite_note-259][^cite_note-260]

There is relatively little variation between human geographical populations, and most of the variation that occurs is at the individual level.[^cite_note-roberts1-255][^cite_note-261][^cite_note-262] Much of human variation is continuous, often with no clear points of demarcation.[^cite_note-263][^cite_note-264][^cite_note-Jablonski2004-256][^cite_note-265] Genetic data shows that no matter how population groups are defined, two people from the same population group are almost as different from each other as two people from any two different population groups.[^cite_note-genetic_variation1-178][^cite_note-266][^cite_note-267] Dark-skinned populations that are found in Africa, Australia, and South Asia are not closely related to each other.[^cite_note-Jablonski2004-256][^cite_note-268]

Genetic research has demonstrated that human populations native to the [African continent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_continent) are the most genetically diverse[^cite_note-269] and genetic diversity decreases with migratory distance from Africa, possibly the result of [bottlenecks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_bottleneck) during human migration.[^cite_note-270][^cite_note-271] These non-African populations acquired new genetic inputs from local [admixture with archaic populations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interbreeding_between_archaic_and_modern_humans) and have much greater variation from Neanderthals and [Denisovans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denisovans) than is found in Africa,[^cite_note-Bergstrom2020-189] though Neanderthal admixture into African populations may be underestimated.[^cite_note-272] Furthermore, recent studies have found that populations in [sub-Saharan Africa](27067.md), and particularly [West Africa](67393.md), have ancestral genetic variation which predates modern humans and has been lost in most non-African populations. Some of this ancestry is thought to originate from admixture with an [unknown archaic hominin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interbreeding_between_archaic_and_modern_humans#Archaic_African_hominins) that diverged before the split of Neanderthals and modern humans.[^cite_note-Bergstrom2020-189][^cite_note-273]

Humans are a [gonochoric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gonochorism) species, meaning they are divided into male and female [sexes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biological_sex).[^cite_note-274][^cite_note-275][^cite_note-276] The greatest degree of genetic [variation exists between males and females](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_differences_in_humans). While the [nucleotide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleotide_diversity) genetic variation of individuals of the same sex across global populations is no greater than 0.1%–0.5%, the genetic difference between [males](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Man) and [females](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woman) is between 1% and 2%. Males on average are 15% heavier and 15 cm (6 in) taller than females.[^cite_note-277][^cite_note-278] On average, males have about 40–50% more upper-body strength and 20–30% more lower-body strength than females at the same weight, due to higher amounts of muscle and larger muscle fibers.[^cite_note-279] Females generally have a higher [body fat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Body_fat) percentage than males.[^cite_note-280] Females have [lighter skin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_skin_color#Sexual_dimorphism) than males of the same population; this has been explained by a higher need for vitamin D in females during pregnancy and [lactation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lactation).[^cite_note-281] As there are chromosomal differences between females and males, some X- and Y-chromosome-related conditions and [disorders](8072.md) only affect either males or females.[^cite_note-282] After allowing for body weight and volume, the male voice is usually an [octave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Octave) deeper than the female voice.[^cite_note-283] Females have a [longer life span](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_differences_in_longevity) in almost every population around the world.[^cite_note-284] There are [intersex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intersex) variations in the human population; however, these are rare.[^cite_note-285][^cite_note-286] Estimates range from 0.018% to 1.7%.

## Psychology

Main article: [Psychology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychology)



Drawing of the [human brain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_brain), showing several important structures

The [human brain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_brain) is the locus of "higher" order functioning such as [thought](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thought), [reasoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reason), and [abstraction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abstraction).[^cite_note-287] These [cognitive processes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mental_function) constitute the [mind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mind), and, along with their [behavioral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Behavior) consequences, are studied in the field of [psychology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychology). Humans have a larger and more developed [prefrontal cortex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prefrontal_cortex) than other primates, the region of the brain associated with higher [cognition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognition).[^cite_note-288][^cite_note-289] This has led humans to proclaim themselves to be more intelligent than any other known species.[^cite_note-290] Objectively defining intelligence is difficult, with other animals adapting senses and excelling in areas that humans are unable to.[^cite_note-291]

There are some traits that, although not strictly unique, do set humans apart from other animals.[^cite_note-292] Humans may be the only animals who have [episodic memory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episodic_memory#In_animals) and who can engage in "[mental time travel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mental_time_travel#Evolution_and_human_uniqueness)".[^cite_note-293] Even compared with other social animals, humans have an unusually high degree of flexibility in their facial expressions.[^cite_note-294] Humans are the only animals known to cry emotional tears.[^cite_note-295] Humans are one of the few animals able to self-recognize in [mirror tests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirror_test),[^cite_note-296] and there is also debate over to what extent humans are the only animals with a [theory of mind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theory_of_mind).[^cite_note-297][^cite_note-298]

### Sleep and dreaming

Main articles: [Sleep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sleep) and [Dream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dream)

Humans are generally [diurnal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diurnality). The average sleep requirement is between seven and nine hours per day for an adult and nine to ten hours per day for a child; elderly people usually sleep for six to seven hours. Having less sleep than this is common among humans, even though [sleep deprivation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sleep_deprivation) can have negative health effects. A sustained restriction of adult sleep to four hours per day has been shown to correlate with changes in physiology and mental state, including reduced memory, fatigue, aggression, and bodily discomfort.[^cite_note-299]

During sleep humans dream, where they experience sensory images and sounds. Dreaming is stimulated by the [pons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pons) and mostly occurs during the [rapid eye movement (REM) phase of sleep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/REM_phase_of_sleep).[^cite_note-300] The length of a dream can vary, from a few seconds up to 30 minutes.[^cite_note-301] Humans have three to five dreams per night, and some may have up to seven.[^cite_note-302] Dreamers are more likely to remember the dream if awakened during the REM phase. The events in dreams are generally outside the control of the dreamer, with the exception of [lucid dreaming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucid_dream), where the dreamer is [self-aware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-aware).[^cite_note-303] Dreams can at times make a [creative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creativity) thought occur or give a sense of [inspiration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artistic_inspiration),[^cite_note-304] [as is the case with](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_works_based_on_dreams) some notable works of fiction, scientific concepts, and art.

### Consciousness and thought

Main articles: [Consciousness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consciousness) and [Cognition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognition)

Human consciousness, at its simplest, is [sentience](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sentience) or [awareness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Awareness) of internal or external existence.[^cite_note-305] Despite centuries of analyses, definitions, explanations and debates by philosophers and scientists, the underlying nature of consciousness remains enigmatic and poorly understood,[^cite_note-306] being "at once the most familiar and most mysterious aspect of our lives".[^cite_note-307] The only widely agreed notion about the topic is the intuition that it exists.[^cite_note-308] Opinions differ about what exactly needs to be studied and explained as consciousness. Some philosophers divide consciousness into phenomenal consciousness, which is sensory experience itself, and access consciousness, which can be used for reasoning or directly controlling actions.[^cite_note-309] It is sometimes synonymous with 'the mind', and at other times, an aspect of it. Historically it is associated with [introspection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Introspection), private [thought](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thought), [imagination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imagination), and [volition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volition_\(psychology\)).[^cite_note-310] It now often includes some kind of [experience](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Experience), [cognition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognition), [feeling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feeling) or [perception](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perception). It may be 'awareness', or '[awareness of awareness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meta-cognition)', or [self-awareness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-awareness).[^cite_note-311] There might be different levels or [orders of consciousness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Higher-order_theories_of_consciousness),[^cite_note-312] or different kinds of consciousness, or just one kind with different features.[^cite_note-313]

The process of acquiring knowledge and understanding through thought, experience, and the senses is known as cognition.[^cite_note-314] The human brain [perceives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perception) the external world through the [senses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sense), and each individual human is influenced greatly by their experiences, leading to [subjective](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subjectivity) views of [existence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Existence) and the passage of time.[^cite_note-315] The nature of thought is central to psychology and related fields. [Cognitive psychology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive_psychology) studies [cognition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognition), the [mental processes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mental_function) underlying behavior.[^cite_note-316] Largely focusing on the development of the human mind through the life span, [developmental psychology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developmental_psychology) seeks to understand how people come to perceive, understand, and act within the world and how these processes change as they age.[^cite_note-317][^cite_note-318] This may focus on intellectual, cognitive, neural, social, or [moral development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moral_development). [Psychologists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychologists) have developed intelligence tests and the concept of [intelligence quotient](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intelligence_quotient) in order to assess the relative intelligence of human beings and study its [distribution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Distribution_\(mathematics\)) among the population.[^cite_note-319]

### Motivation and emotion

Main articles: [Motivation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motivation) and [Emotion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emotion)



Illustration of grief from [Charles Darwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Darwin)'s 1872 book *[The Expression of the Emotions in Man and Animals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Expression_of_the_Emotions_in_Man_and_Animals)*

Human motivation is not yet wholly understood.
