From a psychological perspective, [Maslow's hierarchy of needs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maslow's_hierarchy_of_needs) is a well-established theory that can be defined as the process of satisfying certain needs in ascending order of complexity.[^cite_note-320] From a more general, philosophical perspective, human motivation can be defined as a commitment to, or withdrawal from, various goals requiring the application of human ability. Furthermore, [incentive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incentive) and [preference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preference) are both factors, as are any perceived links between incentives and preferences. [Volition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volition_\(psychology\)) may also be involved, in which case willpower is also a factor. Ideally, both motivation and volition ensure the selection, striving for, and [realization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Realisation_\(metrology\)) of goals in an optimal manner, a [function](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Function_\(biology\)) beginning in childhood and continuing throughout a lifetime in a process known as [socialization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialization).[^cite_note-321]

Emotions are [biological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biological) states associated with the nervous system[^cite_note-322][^cite_note-323] brought on by [neurophysiological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neurophysiology) changes variously associated with thoughts, feelings, behavioral responses, and a degree of [pleasure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pleasure) or [displeasure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suffering).[^cite_note-324][^cite_note-325] They are often [intertwined](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reciprocal_influence) with [mood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mood_\(psychology\)), [temperament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperament), [personality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Personality_psychology), [disposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disposition), [creativity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creativity),[^cite_note-326] and motivation. Emotion has a significant influence on human behavior and their ability to learn.[^cite_note-327] Acting on extreme or uncontrolled emotions can lead to social disorder and crime,[^cite_note-328] with studies showing criminals may have lower [emotional intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emotional_intelligence) than normal.[^cite_note-329]

Emotional experiences perceived as [pleasant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pleasure), such as [joy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joy), [interest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interest_\(emotion\)), or [contentment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contentment), contrast with those perceived as [unpleasant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suffering), like [anxiety](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anxiety), [sadness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sadness), [anger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anger), and [despair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Depression_\(mood\)).[^cite_note-330] [Happiness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Happiness), or the state of being happy, is a human emotional condition. The definition of happiness is a common philosophical topic. Some define it as experiencing the [feeling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feeling) of positive [emotional affects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affect_\(psychology\)), while avoiding the negative ones.[^cite_note-331][^cite_note-332] Others see it as an appraisal of [life satisfaction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_satisfaction) or [quality of life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quality_of_life).[^cite_note-333] Recent research suggests that being happy might involve experiencing some negative emotions when humans feel they are warranted.[^cite_note-334]

### Sexuality and love

Main articles: [Human sexuality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_sexuality) and [Love](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Love)



Human parents often display [familial love](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Familial_love) for their children.

For humans, sexuality involves [biological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biological), [erotic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erotic), [physical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physical_intimacy), [emotional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emotional_intimacy), [social](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social), or [spiritual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spirituality) feelings and behaviors.[^cite_note-S._Greenberg-335][^cite_note-Bolin-336] Because it is a broad term that has varied with historical contexts over time, it lacks a precise definition.[^cite_note-Bolin-336] The biological and physical aspects of sexuality largely concern the [human reproductive functions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_reproduction), including the [human sexual response cycle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_sexual_response_cycle).[^cite_note-S._Greenberg-335][^cite_note-Bolin-336] Sexuality also affects and is affected by cultural, political, legal, philosophical, [moral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morality), [ethical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethical), and religious aspects of life.[^cite_note-S._Greenberg-335][^cite_note-Bolin-336] Sexual desire ([libido](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libido)) is a basic mental state present at the beginning of sexual behavior. Studies have found that men report stronger sexual desire than women and [masturbate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masturbation) more frequently.[^cite_note-337][^cite_note-338]

Humans can fall anywhere along a continuous scale of [sexual orientation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_orientation),[^cite_note-339] although most humans are [heterosexual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heterosexual).[^cite_note-Bailey16-340][^cite_note-LeVay-341] While [homosexual](5488304.md) behavior [occurs in some other animals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homosexual_behavior_in_animals), only humans and [domestic sheep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheep) have so far been found to exhibit exclusive preference for same-sex relationships.[^cite_note-Bailey16-340] Most evidence supports nonsocial, [biological causes of sexual orientation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biology_and_sexual_orientation),[^cite_note-Bailey16-340] as cultures that are very tolerant of homosexuality do not have significantly higher rates of it.[^cite_note-LeVay-341][^cite_note-342] Research in [neuroscience](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neuroscience) and [genetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetics) suggests that other aspects of human sexuality are biologically influenced as well.[^cite_note-343]

Love most commonly refers to a feeling of strong attraction or emotional [attachment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attachment_\(psychology\)). It can be impersonal (the love of an object, ideal, or strong political or spiritual connection) or interpersonal (love between humans).[^cite_note-344] In the emotional state of [romantic love](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romantic_love), neurochemicals such as [dopamine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dopamine), [norepinephrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norepinephrine), [serotonin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serotonin), and [oxytocin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxytocin) stimulate the brain's [reward system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reward_system) through the [mesocorticolimbic pathway (MCL)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dopaminergic_pathways). This [dopaminergic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dopaminergic) pathway may result in side effects such as [tachycardia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tachycardia) (increased [heart rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heart_rate)), loss of appetite, [insomnia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insomnia) ([sleeplessness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sleep_deprivation)), and [intense euphoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euphoria).[^cite_note-345]

## Culture

Main articles: [Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture) and [Cultural universal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_universal)

| Most widely spoken languages[^cite_note-346][^cite_note-CIAWorldFactbook-122] | [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language), [Mandarin Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandarin_Chinese), [Hindi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindi), [Spanish](26825.md), [Standard Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_Arabic), [Bengali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_language), [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language), [Russian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language), [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_language), [Urdu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urdu) |
| --- | --- |
| Most practiced religions[^cite_note-CIAWorldFactbook-122][^cite_note-Pew2017-347] | [Christianity](5211.md), [Islam](6037917.md), [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism), [Buddhism](3267529.md), [folk religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_religion), [Sikhism](27964.md), [Judaism](15624.md), [unaffiliated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion) |

Humanity's large set of intellectual skills was a key factor in the species' eventual technological advancement and concomitant domination of the biosphere.[^cite_note-348] Disregarding extinct hominids, humans are the only animals known to teach generalizable information,[^cite_note-349] innately deploy recursive [embedding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dependent_clause) to generate and communicate complex concepts,[^cite_note-350] engage in the "[folk physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folk_physics)" required for competent tool design,[^cite_note-351][^cite_note-352] or cook food in the wild.[^cite_note-353] The ability to preserve and teach information to future generations in one or more societies is known as the [ratchet effect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ratchet_effect) in [anthropology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthropology).[^cite_note-\:0-2] According to the metaphor, once a group of people understands a technology, it is hard for them to unlearn it, and it may be used to learn technologies of further complexity.[^cite_note-\:0-2] Teaching and learning preserves the cultural and ethnographic identity of human societies.[^cite_note-354] Other traits and behaviors that are mostly unique to humans include starting fires,[^cite_note-355] [phoneme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoneme) structuring[^cite_note-356] and [vocal learning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vocal_learning).[^cite_note-357]

### Language

Main article: [Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language)



Principal [language families](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_language_families) of the world (and in some cases geographic groups of families)

While many species [communicate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_communication), [language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language) is unique to humans,[^cite_note-358] a defining feature of humanity,[^cite_note-359][^cite_note-360] and a [cultural universal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_universal).[^cite_note-361] Unlike the limited communication systems of other animals, human language is open – an infinite number of meanings can be produced by combining a limited number of symbols.[^cite_note-362][^cite_note-363] Human language also has the capacity of [displacement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Displacement_\(linguistics\)), using words to represent things and happenings that are not presently or locally occurring but reside in the shared imagination of interlocutors.[^cite_note-Revolution-155]

Language differs from other forms of communication in that it is [modality independent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Origin_of_speech#Modality-independence); people can convey the same meanings through different media: audibly in [speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speech), visually by [sign language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sign_language) or writing, and through tactile media such as [braille](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Braille).[^cite_note-364] Language is central to communication between humans, and to the sense of [identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Identity_\(social_science\)) that can unite nations, cultures, and ethnic groups.[^cite_note-365]

As of 1996, humans used approximately six thousand different languages, including sign languages, and many thousands more are [extinct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extinct_language).[^cite_note-366]

### The arts

Main article: [The arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_arts)

&#x20;Human arts can take many forms, including [visual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visual_arts), [literary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literary_arts), and [performing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Performing_arts). Visual art can range from [paintings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Painting) and [sculptures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sculpture) to [film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Film), [fashion design](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fashion_design), and [architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture).[^cite_note-367] Literary arts can include [prose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prose), [poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poetry), and [dramas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drama). The performing arts generally involve [theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theater), [music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music), and [dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dance).[^cite_note-368][^cite_note-369] Humans often combine the different forms (for example, music videos).[^cite_note-370] Other entities that have been described as having artistic qualities include [food preparation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culinary_arts), [video games](5363.md), and [medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicine).[^cite_note-371][^cite_note-372][^cite_note-373] As well as providing entertainment and transferring knowledge, the arts are also used for [political purposes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_arts_and_politics).[^cite_note-374]&#x20;



The [Deluge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deluge_\(mythology\)) tablet of [the *Gilgamesh* epic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epic_of_Gilgamesh) in [Akkadian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akkadian_language)

[Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art) is a defining characteristic of humans, and there is evidence for a relationship between creativity and language.[^cite_note-Morriss-Kay-2010-375] The earliest evidence of art was shell engravings made by *Homo erectus* 300,000 years before modern humans evolved.[^cite_note-376] Art attributed to *H. sapiens* existed at least 75,000 years ago, with jewelry and drawings found in caves in South Africa.[^cite_note-377][^cite_note-378] There are various hypotheses as to why humans have [adapted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adaptation) to the arts. These include allowing them to better problem-solve issues, providing a means to control or influence other humans, encouraging cooperation and contribution within a society, or increasing the chance of attracting a potential mate.[^cite_note-379] The use of imagination developed through art, combined with logic, may have given early humans an evolutionary advantage.[^cite_note-Morriss-Kay-2010-375]

Evidence of humans engaging in musical activities predates cave art, and so far music has been [practiced by virtually all known human cultures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_universal).[^cite_note-Morley-2014-380] There exists a wide variety of [music genres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_genre) and [ethnic musics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_music), with humans' musical abilities being related to other abilities, including complex social human behaviors.[^cite_note-Morley-2014-380] It has been shown that human brains respond to music by becoming synchronized with the rhythm and beat, a process called [entrainment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entrainment_\(biomusicology\)).[^cite_note-381] Dance is also a form of human expression found in all cultures[^cite_note-382] and may have evolved as a way to help early humans communicate.[^cite_note-383] Listening to music and observing dance stimulates the [orbitofrontal cortex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbitofrontal_cortex) and other pleasure-sensing areas of the brain.[^cite_note-384]

Unlike speaking, reading and writing do not come naturally to humans and must be taught.[^cite_note-385] Still, [literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literature) has been present before the invention of words and language, with 30,000-year-old paintings on walls inside some caves portraying a series of dramatic scenes.[^cite_note-Puchner-386] One of the oldest surviving works of literature is the *[Epic of Gilgamesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epic_of_Gilgamesh)*, first engraved on ancient [Babylonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylonia) tablets about 4,000 years ago.[^cite_note-387] Beyond simply passing down knowledge, the use and sharing of imaginative [fiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiction) through stories might have helped develop humans' capabilities for communication and increased the likelihood of securing a mate.[^cite_note-388] Storytelling may also be used as a way to provide the audience with moral lessons and encourage cooperation.[^cite_note-Puchner-386]

### Tools and technologies

Main articles: [Tool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tool) and [Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technology)

Train running on a track

The [SCMaglev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SCMaglev), the [fastest train](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_speed_record_for_rail_vehicles) in the world, clocking in at 603 km/h (375 mph) as of 2015

[^cite_note-389]

Stone tools were used by [proto-humans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-humans) at least 2.5 million years ago.[^cite_note-390] The use and manufacture of tools has been put forward as the ability that defines humans more than anything else[^cite_note-Choi-2009-391] and has historically been seen as an important evolutionary step.[^cite_note-392] The technology became much more sophisticated about 1.8 million years ago,[^cite_note-Choi-2009-391] with the [controlled use of fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Control_of_fire_by_early_humans) beginning around 1 million years ago.[^cite_note-393][^cite_note-394] The wheel and wheeled vehicles appeared simultaneously in several regions sometime in the fourth millennium BC.[^cite_note-Bodnár-2018-63] The development of more complex tools and technologies allowed land to be [cultivated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arable_land) and animals to be [domesticated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestication), thus proving essential in the development of [agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture) – what is known as the [Neolithic Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic_Revolution).[^cite_note-395]

[China](5405.md) developed [paper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paper), the [printing press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Printing_press), [gunpowder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gunpowder), the [compass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compass) and [other important inventions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Chinese_inventions).[^cite_note-396] The continued improvements in [smelting](28716.md) allowed the [forging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forging) of copper, bronze, iron, and eventually [steel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steel)—which is used in [railways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Railways), [skyscrapers](29485.md), and many other products.[^cite_note-397] This coincided with the [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution), where the invention of automated machines brought major changes to humans' lifestyles.[^cite_note-398] Modern technology is observed as [progressing exponentially](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accelerating_change),[^cite_note-399] with major innovations in the 20th century including: [electricity](9540.md), [penicillin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penicillin), [semiconductors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semiconductor), [internal combustion engines](41228673.md), the [Internet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet), [nitrogen-fixing fertilizers](37401.md), [airplanes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airplane), [computers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer), [automobiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Car), [contraceptive pills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combined_oral_contraceptive_pill), [nuclear fission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_fission), the [green revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Revolution), [radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio), scientific [plant breeding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plant_breeding), [rockets](26301.md), [air conditioning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_conditioning), [television](29831.md) and the [assembly line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assembly_line).[^cite_note-400]

### Religion and spirituality

Main articles: [Religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion) and [Spirituality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spirituality)



[Shango](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shango), the [Orisha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orisha) of fire, lightning, and thunder, in the [Yoruba religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoruba_religion), depicted on horseback

[Definitions of religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Definition_of_religion) vary;[^cite_note-Idinopulos-1998-401] according to one definition, a religion is a [belief](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belief) system concerning the [supernatural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supernatural), [sacred](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacred) or [divine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divinity), and practices, [values](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Values), institutions and [rituals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ritual) associated with such belief. Some religions also have a [moral code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moral_code). The [evolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_psychology_of_religion) and the history of the [first religions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_origin_of_religions) have become areas of active scientific investigation.[^cite_note-402][^cite_note-403][^cite_note-404] Credible evidence of religious behavior dates to the [Middle Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Paleolithic) era (45–200 [thousand years ago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tya_\(unit\))).[^cite_note-405] It may have evolved to play a role in helping enforce and encourage cooperation between humans.[^cite_note-406]

Religion manifests in diverse forms.[^cite_note-Idinopulos-1998-401] Religion can include a belief in [life after death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_after_death),[^cite_note-407] the [origin of life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Origin_of_life), the nature of the [universe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universe) ([religious cosmology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_cosmology)) and its [ultimate fate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultimate_fate) ([eschatology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eschatology)), and [moral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morality) or [ethical teachings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_ethics).[^cite_note-408] Views on [transcendence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcendence_\(religion\)) and [immanence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immanence) vary substantially; traditions variously espouse [monism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monism), [deism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deism), [pantheism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pantheism), and [theism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theism) (including [polytheism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polytheism) and [monotheism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monotheism)).[^cite_note-409]

Although measuring religiosity is difficult,[^cite_note-410] a majority of humans profess some variety of religious or spiritual belief.[^cite_note-411] In 2015, the plurality was [Christian](18337522.md), followed by [Muslim](19541.md), [Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindus), and [Buddhist](3267529.md).[^cite_note-412] As of 2015, about 16%, or slightly under 1.2 billion humans, were [irreligious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligious), including those with no religious beliefs or no identity with any religion.[^cite_note-Pew2017-347]

### Science and philosophy

Main articles: [Science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science) and [Philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophy)



The [Dunhuang map](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dunhuang_map), a [star map](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Star_map) showing the North Polar region. China circa 700.

A defining attribute of humanity is the capacity for [cumulative cultural evolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_evolution) by [transmitting knowledge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knowledge_transfer) across generations by continuously building on past knowledge to develop tools, [scientific laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_law), and other advancements to pass on to future generations.[^cite_note-413] This accumulated knowledge can be tested to answer various questions or make predictions about how the universe functions, driving ongoing human advancement.[^cite_note-414] This phenomenon is known as the ratchet effect in anthropology.[^cite_note-\:0-2]

[Aristotle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle) has been described as the first scientist,[^cite_note-415] and preceded the rise of scientific thought through the [Hellenistic period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_period).[^cite_note-416] Other early advances in science came from the [Han dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_and_technology_of_the_Han_dynasty) in China and during the [Islamic Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Golden_Age).[^cite_note-417][^cite_note-Renima-2016-89] The [Scientific Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_Revolution), near the end of the [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance), led to the emergence of [modern science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_science).[^cite_note-418]

A chain of events and influences led to the development of the [scientific method](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method), a process of observation and experimentation that is used to differentiate science from [pseudoscience](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pseudoscience).[^cite_note-419] An understanding of [mathematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathematics) is unique to humans, although other species of animals have some [numerical cognition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Numerical_cognition).[^cite_note-420] All of science can be divided into three major branches, the [formal sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formal_sciences) (e.g., [logic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logic) and mathematics), which are concerned with [formal systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formal_systems), the [applied sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Applied_sciences) (e.g., engineering, medicine), which are focused on practical applications, and the empirical sciences, which are based on [empirical observation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empirical_observation) and are in turn divided into [natural sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_sciences) (e.g., [physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physics), [chemistry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemistry), [biology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biology)) and [social sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_sciences) (e.g., [psychology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychology), economics, sociology).[^cite_note-421]

Philosophy is a field of study where humans seek to understand fundamental truths about themselves and the world in which they live.[^cite_note-422] Philosophical inquiry has been a major feature in the development of humans' intellectual history.[^cite_note-423] It has been described as the "no man's land" between definitive scientific knowledge and dogmatic religious teachings.[^cite_note-424] Major fields of philosophy include [metaphysics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metaphysics), [epistemology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epistemology), [logic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logic_\(philosophy\)), and [axiology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axiology) (which includes [ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics) and [aesthetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aesthetics)).[^cite_note-425]

## Society

Main article: [Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Society)



Photo taken between 1890 and 1923 of an [indigenous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas) family in [Brazil](3383.md), consisting of three adolescent or adult females, an adult male, and a baby. Humans often live in family-based social structures.

Society is the system of [organizations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization) and [institutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institution) arising from interaction between humans. Humans are highly social and tend to live in large complex social groups. They can be divided into different groups according to their [income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income), [wealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wealth), [power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_\(social_and_political\)), [reputation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reputation), and other factors. The structure of [social stratification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_stratification) and the degree of [social mobility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_mobility) differ, especially between modern and traditional societies.[^cite_note-426] Human groups range from the size of [families](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family) to [nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation). The first form of human social organization is thought to have resembled [hunter-gatherer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter-gatherer) [band societies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Band_society).[^cite_note-427]

### Gender

Main article: [Gender](38076.md)



A [man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Man) as the [groom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bridegroom) and a [woman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woman) as the [bride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bride) in a [Hindu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu) North Indian wedding, the groom wearing a [sherwani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sherwani) and [pagri turban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pagri_\(turban\)) and the bride wearing a [sari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sari).

Human societies typically exhibit [gender identities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_identity) and [gender roles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_role) that distinguish between [masculine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masculinity) and [feminine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Femininity) characteristics and prescribe the range of acceptable behaviors and attitudes for their members based on their sex.[^cite_note-428][^cite_note-429] The most common categorization is a [gender binary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_binary) of [men](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Men) and [women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women).[^cite_note-430] Some societies recognize a [third gender](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_gender),[^cite_note-431] or less commonly a fourth or fifth.[^cite_note-432][^cite_note-433] In some other societies, [non-binary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-binary_gender) is used as an umbrella term for a range of gender identities that are not solely male or female.[^cite_note-434]

Gender roles are often associated with a division of [norms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_norm), [practices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Practice_\(social_theory\)), [dress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clothing), [behavior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_behavior), [rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rights), [duties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duty), [privileges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privilege_\(social_inequality\)), [status](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_status), and [power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_\(social_and_political\)), with men enjoying more rights and privileges than women in most societies, both today and in the past.[^cite_note-435] As a [social construct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_constructionism),[^cite_note-436] gender roles are not fixed and vary historically within a society. Challenges to predominant gender norms have recurred in many societies.[^cite_note-437][^cite_note-438] Little is known about gender roles in the earliest human societies. [Early modern humans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_modern_human) probably had a range of gender roles similar to that of modern cultures from at least the [Upper Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Paleolithic), while the Neanderthals were less sexually dimorphic and there is evidence that the behavioral difference between males and females was minimal.[^cite_note-439]

### Kinship

Main article: [Kinship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kinship)

All human societies organize, recognize, and classify types of social relationships based on relations between parents, children, and other descendants ([consanguinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consanguinity)), and relations through [marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marriage) ([affinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affinity_\(law\))). There is also a third type applied to [godparents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Godparent) or [adoptive children](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adoption) ([fictive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fictive_kinship)). These culturally defined relationships are referred to as kinship. In many societies, it is one of the most important social organizing principles and plays a role in transmitting status and [inheritance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inheritance).[^cite_note-440] All societies have rules of [incest taboo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incest_taboo), according to which marriage between certain kinds of kin relations is prohibited, and some also have rules of preferential marriage with certain kin relations.[^cite_note-441]

Pair bonding is a ubiquitous feature of human sexual relationships, whether it is manifested as serial monogamy, [polygyny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polygyny), or [polyandry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polyandry).[^cite_note-442] Genetic evidence indicates that humans were predominantly polygynous for most of their existence as a species, but that this began to shift during the Neolithic, when [monogamy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monogamy) started becoming widespread concomitantly with the transition from nomadic to sedentary societies.[^cite_note-443] Anatomical evidence in the form of second-to-fourth digit ratios, a biomarker for prenatal androgen effects, likewise indicates modern humans were polygynous during the Pleistocene.[^cite_note-444]

### Ethnicity

Main article: [Ethnic group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_group)

Human ethnic groups are a social category that [identifies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Identity_\(social_science\)) people together based on shared attributes that distinguish them from other groups. These can be a common set of traditions, [ancestry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancestry), [language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language), [history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History), [society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Society), [culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture), [nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation), [religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion), or social treatment within their residing area.[^cite_note-445][^cite_note-446] Ethnicity is separate from the concept of [race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_\(human_categorization\)), which is based on physical characteristics, although both are [socially constructed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_constructionism).[^cite_note-447] Assigning ethnicity to a certain population is complicated, as even within common ethnic designations there can be a diverse range of subgroups, and the makeup of these ethnic groups can change over time at both the collective and individual level.[^cite_note-REGWG2005-180] Also, there is no generally accepted definition of what constitutes an ethnic group.[^cite_note-448] Ethnic groupings can play a powerful role in the [social identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_identity) and solidarity of ethnopolitical units. This has been closely tied to the rise of the [nation state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation_state) as the predominant form of political organization in the 19th and 20th centuries.[^cite_note-449][^cite_note-450][^cite_note-451]

### Government and politics

Main articles: [Government](12229.md) and [Politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics)



The [United Nations headquarters](188955.md) (left) in New York City, which houses one of the world's largest political organizations

As farming populations gathered in larger and denser communities, interactions between these different groups increased. This led to the development of governance within and between the communities.[^cite_note-452] Humans have evolved the ability to change affiliation with various social groups relatively easily, including previously strong political alliances, if doing so is seen as providing personal advantages.[^cite_note-453] This [cognitive flexibility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive_flexibility) allows individual humans to change their political ideologies, with those with higher flexibility less likely to support authoritarian and nationalistic stances.[^cite_note-454]

Governments create [laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law) and [policies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Policies) that affect the citizens they govern. There have been [many forms of government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_forms_of_government) throughout human history, each having various means of obtaining power and the ability to exert diverse controls on the population.[^cite_note-455] Approximately 47% of humans live in some form of a [democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy), 17% in a [hybrid regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hybrid_regime), and 37% in an [authoritarian regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarian_regime).[^cite_note-456] Many countries belong to [international organizations](18201224.md) and [alliances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alliances); the largest of these is the [United Nations](31769.md), with [193 member states](31969.md).[^cite_note-457]

### Trade and economics

Main articles: [Trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade) and [Economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economics)



The [Silk Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silk_Road) (red) and spice [trade routes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_routes) (blue)

Trade, the voluntary exchange of goods and services, is seen as a characteristic that differentiates humans from other animals and has been cited as a practice that gave *Homo sapiens* a major advantage over other hominids.[^cite_note-458] Evidence suggests early *H. sapiens* made use of long-distance trade routes to exchange goods and ideas, leading to [cultural explosions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_explosion) and providing additional food sources when hunting was sparse, while such trade networks did not exist for the now extinct Neanderthals.[^cite_note-459][^cite_note-460] Early trade likely involved materials for creating tools like [obsidian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obsidian).[^cite_note-461] The first truly international trade routes were around the [spice trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spice_trade) through the Roman and medieval periods.[^cite_note-462]

Early human [economies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy) were more likely to be based around [gift-giving](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gift_economy) instead of a [bartering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barter) system.[^cite_note-463] Early [money](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Money) consisted of [commodities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commodity_money); the oldest being in the form of cattle and the most widely used being [cowrie shells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cowrie_shells).\[^cite_note-[www.pbs.org-1996-464](http://www.pbs.org-1996-464/)] Money has since evolved into government-issued [coins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coins), [paper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paper_money), and [electronic money](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_money).\[^cite_note-[www.pbs.org-1996-464](http://www.pbs.org-1996-464/)] Human study of economics is a [social science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_science) that looks at how societies distribute scarce resources among different people.[^cite_note-465] There are massive [inequalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_inequality) in the division of [wealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wealth) among humans; the eight richest humans are worth the same monetary value as the poorest half of all the human population.[^cite_note-466]

### Conflict

Main article: [Conflict (process)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conflict_\(process\))

Further information: [Violent crime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violent_crime) and [War](33158.md)



American troops [landing at Normandy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normandy_landings), WWII

Humans commit violence on other humans at a rate comparable to other primates, but have an increased preference for killing adults, [infanticide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infanticide_\(zoology\)) being more common among other primates.[^cite_note-467] Phylogenetic analysis predicts that 2% of early *H. sapiens* would be [murdered](20171.md), rising to 12% during the medieval period, before dropping to below 2% in modern times.[^cite_note-468] There is great variation in violence between human populations, with rates of homicide about 0.01% in societies that have [legal systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_national_legal_systems) and strong cultural attitudes against violence.[^cite_note-469]

The willingness of humans to kill other members of their species en masse through organized conflict (i.e., [war](33158.md)) has long been the subject of debate. One school of thought holds that war evolved as a means to eliminate competitors, and has always been an innate human characteristic.[^cite_note-PhilipWalkerBioarchaeologicalPerspectiveOnViolenceAndWar-470] Another suggests that war is a relatively recent phenomenon and has appeared due to changing social conditions.[^cite_note-471] While not settled, current evidence indicates that interpersonal violence has been a ubiquitous part of human history since the beginnings of the species' existence, with the bioarchaeological record showing significant incidence of violent trauma believed to be a result of war in hunter-gatherer populations long before the emergence of agriculture and settled societies.[^cite_note-PhilipWalkerBioarchaeologicalPerspectiveOnViolenceAndWar-470] War has had a high cost on human life; it is estimated that during the 20th century, between 167 million and 188 million people died as a result of war.[^cite_note-472] War casualty data is less reliable for pre-medieval times, especially global figures. But compared with any period over the past 600 years, the past 80 years (post-1945) have seen a very significant drop in global military and civilian death rates due to armed conflict.[^cite_note-473]

The changes that occur during puberty usually happen in an ordered sequence, beginning with thelarche (breast development) at around age 10 or 11, followed by adrenarche (growth of pubic hair due to androgen stimulation), peak height velocity, and finally menarche (the onset of menses), which usually occurs around age 12 or 13.

[Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20230110011826/https://books.google.com/books?id=84StBAAAQBAJ\&pg=PA18) from the original on 10 January 2023. Retrieved 30 July 2022. On average, the onset of puberty is about 18 months earlier for girls (usually starting around the age of 10 or 11 and lasting until they are 15 to 17) than for boys (who usually begin puberty at about the age of 11 to 12 and complete it by the age of 16 to 17, on average).

Retrieved 30 July 2022. I would suggest that when we talk about happiness, we are actually referring, much of the time, to a complex emotional phenomenon. Call it emotional well-being. Happiness as emotional well-being concerns your emotions and moods, more broadly your emotional condition as a whole. To be happy is to inhabit a favorable emotional state.... On this view, we can think of happiness, loosely, as the opposite of anxiety and depression. Being in good spirits, quick to laugh and slow to anger, at peace and untroubled, confident and comfortable in your own skin, engaged, energetic and full of life.

[^cite_note-446]: &#x20;People J, Bailey G (2010). *Humanity: An Introduction to Cultural Anthropology* (9th ed.). Wadsworth Cengage learning. p. 389. In essence, an ethnic group is a named social category of people based on perceptions of shared social experience or one's ancestors' experiences. Members of the ethnic group see themselves as sharing cultural traditions and history that distinguish them from other groups. Ethnic group identity has a strong psychological or emotional component that divides the people of the world into opposing categories of 'us' and 'them.' In contrast to social stratification, which divides and unifies people along a series of horizontal axes based on socioeconomic factors, ethnic identities divide and unify people along a series of vertical axes. Thus, ethnic groups, at least theoretically, cut across socioeconomic class differences, drawing members from all strata of the population.
